Beyond Finite-State Systems

We emphasize that
HyMCA
implements our novel game-based verification approach in its simplest possible form by computing an
explicit-state
parity game.
Our preliminary experiments show that even such a direct implementation performs well compared to previous QBF-based methods.
We stress that the main complexity in A-HLTL verification stems from the expressiveness of the logic itself; the complexity (size) of the system plays a secondary role.
All properties checked in
Tables

1
and
2
use the high-level asynchronous reasoning of A-HLTL, and delegate the search for an appropriate asynchronous stuttering to the verification tool (cf.
Section

8
).
Our prototype is not meant as a full-fledged verification tool.
Instead, it demonstrates that the game-based approach works well in a
finite-state setting
.
A key advantage of our game lies in its applicability to infinite-state systems.
For example, in the domain of
synchronous
HyperLTL,
Coenen et al. 2019b
showed that the synchronous game-based approach works well in a finite-state setting, leading to successful adoption to infinite-state systems; either by solving infinite-state games automatically
(
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022b
;
Itzhaky et al. 2024
)
or interactively
(
Correnson and Finkbeiner 2025
)
.
Our approach handles more general asynchronous properties (including well-known examples like OD), and provides a valuable abstraction: instead of reasoning over infinite traces and stutterings, we only need to reason
locally
over a finite window of states and pointers within that window.
As we argue in
Section

8
, our game thus forms the foundation to extend verification to infinite-state systems.

8.
Related Work

Relational Program Logics.

Relational Hoare Logic

(
Benton 2004
)
– and related Hoare-style hyperproperty specifications
(
D’Osualdo et al. 2022
;
Dardinier and Müller 2024
;
Maillard et al. 2020
;
Gladshtein et al. 2024
;
Assaf et al. 2017
;
Sousa and Dillig 2016
;
Beutner 2024
;
Dickerson et al. 2022
)
– relate the initial and final states of
multiple
program runs and are thus inherently “
asynchronous
” (program executions can take a different number of steps to termination).
However, RHLs struggle to express
temporal
hyperproperties, i.e., properties that reason about the
temporal behavior
along possibly infinite executions (as, e.g., found in infinite protocol interactions or reactive systems), which are needed for properties like
Zdancewic and Myers 2003
’s observational determinism.

⬇

$c$

=

$0$

repeat

@@$o$

=

$
\
lfloor$

$c$

/

$h$

$
\
rfloor$

@@$c$

=

$c$

+

$1$

Figure 7
.
Example program

Hypersafety and Commutativity.

Various works have studied the verification of hypersafety properties by exploiting commutativity
(
Farzan and Vandikas 2020
;
Farzan et al. 2022
;
Eilers et al. 2023
;
Eilers et al. 2020
;
Antonopoulos et al. 2023
;
Shemer et al. 2019
)
.
These approaches attempt to find an alignment of different executions that aids verification by exploiting the fact that we can interleave different program executions.
Our work differs from these approaches in that
quantification over alignments (aka stutterings) is part of the specification
and not just a technique that helps during verification.
Let us take
Zdancewic and Myers 2003
’s observational determinism (OD) as an example, which states that all pairs of traces with (initially) identical low-security input are stutter-equivalent w.r.t. the output.
To verify this property, intermediate program steps, i.e., steps where the output does not change, can be interleaved arbitrarily.
The problem is that one of the verification challenges lies in the
identification
of points where the output changes.

Example 8.1.

Consider the simple integer-based program in
Figure

7
.
Here,
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
rounds down to the nearest integer.
It is easy to see that the output sequence of this program (per loop iteration) is
0^{h}1^{h}2^{h}\cdots
, i.e., for the first
h
loop iterations, the value of
o
is
0
, and so forth.
The output of the program is stutter-equivalent independent of
h
; the program satisfies OD.
Yet, verifying this using commutativity-based frameworks is difficult.
Most existing frameworks
(
Farzan and Vandikas 2020
;
Eilers et al. 2020
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022b
;
Itzhaky et al. 2024
;
Unno et al. 2021
;
Shemer et al. 2019
)
search for
some
alignment that facilitates an easy proof of the property.
To express OD, we do, however, want to compare the output of two executions
whenever the output changes
, i.e., identifying points at which the executions should synchronize is (implicitly) part of the OD specification.
In the above frameworks, we would need to enforce the alignment manually (by, e.g., defining explicit observation points or adding synchronization assertions).
Only
afterward
can the
k
-safety verifier find some alignment (between two points where the output changes) that aids verification.
In our example, we cannot infer the points where the output changes syntactically.
In contrast, A-HLTL offers a richer, higher-level specification language, allowing us to easily express
Zdancewic and Myers 2003
’s OD by using first-class
quantification
over stutterings (see

φ
OD

), i.e., the identification of points that need to be aligned is
part of the specification itself
.

\triangle

Our approach thus differs from existing verification approaches in the expressiveness of the specification language:
Most hypersafety frameworks exclusively focus on the verification challenges stemming from infinite state space, whereas our work focuses on the complexity stemming from the specification logic itself.

Temporal Logics for Asynchronous Hyperproperties.

In recent years, many logics have been proposed for the specification of asynchronous hyperproperties
Examples include A-HLTL (the main object of study in this paper)
(
Baumeister et al. 2021
)
,
H_{\mu}
and mumbling
H_{\mu}
(extensions of the polyadic
\mu
-calculus),
(
Gutsfeld et al. 2021
;
Gutsfeld et al. 2024
)
,
OHyperLTL
(
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022b
)
(an extension of HyperLTL with explicit observation points),
HyperLTL
S

(
Bozzelli et al. 2021
)
(an extension of HyperLTL where operators can skip evaluation steps), HyperLTL
C

(
Bozzelli et al. 2021
)
(an extension of HyperLTL where only some traces progress), variants of team semantics
(
Krebs et al. 2018
;
Gutsfeld et al. 2022
)
, and extensions of first-order logic with explicit quantification over time
(
Bartocci et al. 2022
)
.

Bozzelli et al. 2022
studied the expressiveness of many of these logics and found that most of them are
incomparable
.

Verification of A-HLTL

Baumeister et al. 2021
study a restricted class of A-HLTL properties called
admissible
(see
Section

6.3
).
They show that finite-state model-checking of admissible formulas is decidable, and provide a manual reduction to (synchronous) HyperLTL.

Hsu et al. 2023
study
terminating
systems (see
Section

6.2
) and present a QBF-based bounded model-checking approach.
Unlike previous techniques for A-HLTL, our method is soundly applicable to arbitrary
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
A-HLTL formulas, i.e., it might succeed in verifying a property that does not fall into any known decidable fragment.

\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
and Game-Based Verification.

In recent years, many techniques for verifying hyperproperties
beyond

k
-safety have been proposed
(
Unno et al. 2021
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022b
;
Antonopoulos et al. 2023
;
Itzhaky et al. 2024
)
.
These techniques combine the search for a suitable alignment with the search for witness traces for existential quantifiers.
For example,
Unno et al. 2021
encode verification of
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
properties as a specialized form of Horn clauses and let the solver figure out an appropriate alignment.
Currently, their encoding only searches for
some
alignment that aids verification.
It is an interesting future work to study if the encoding can be extended with
explicit quantification
over alignments (as in A-HLTL).

Correnson et al. 2024
;
Beutner et al. 2024
;
Hsu et al. 2023
employ bounded unrolling to verify or refute
\forall\exists
properties.
Our verification approach is rooted in a game-based interpretation of traces and stutterings.
For
synchronous
HyperLTL properties, such game-based interpretations have been employed successfully to approximate expensive quantifier alternations
(
Coenen et al. 2019b
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022a
)
.
The dynamics of our asynchronous game differ substantially from synchronous games.
Our key idea of using
windows
and
relative pointers
allows the players to have direct control over the stuttering of traces, which is key to approximating A-HLTL’s semantics.
In particular, our asynchronous game allows the players to control the speed (i.e., stuttering) of the traces
individually
.

Itzhaky et al. 2024
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022b
employ a game-based semantics for asynchronous
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
properties expressed in OHyperLTL.
In this logic, the
user
indicates at which points the executions should synchronize, and the verifier attempts to find an alignment between these so-called
observation points
.
As argued before, A-HLTL allows explicit
quantification
over stutterings, which is necessary to succinctly express properties like OD; to express OD in OHyperLTL, we would need to manually place observation points whenever the output changes, which is often not possible syntactically (cf.
Example

8.1
).
It is interesting to see if the CHC encoding by
Itzhaky et al. 2024
can be extended to allow explicit reasoning over stutterings, perhaps using our novel idea of using state windows and relative pointers.

Refinement, Stuttering, and Simulation.

A particularly interesting class of
\forall\exists
hyperproperties are
refinement
properties.
Given two systems
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, and a set of observable variables
\mathit{Obs}
, we can easily express a lock-step refinement property using (synchronous) HyperLTL as
\varphi_{\mathit{refine}}^{\mathit{syn}}:=\forall\pi_{1}\mathpunct{.}\exists\pi_{2}\mathpunct{.}\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\big(\bigwedge_{a\in\mathit{Obs}}a_{\pi_{1}}=a_{\pi_{2}}\big)
.
A popular method for
proving
a refinement property (such as
\varphi_{\mathit{refine}}^{\mathit{syn}}
) is to establish a
simulation relation
between
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}

(
Milner 1971
)
.
A popular technique for automatically
checking
the existence of a (synchronous) simulation relation is to exploit the connection between simulation relations and winning strategies in
simulation games

(
Stirling 1995
)
.
While
\varphi_{\mathit{refine}}^{\mathit{syn}}
requires a lock-step refinement, it often suffices to establish a
stuttering
refinement property (see, e.g.,
(
Leroy 2006
)
), which we can easily express in A-HLTL as

(
\varphi_{\mathit{refine}}
)

\displaystyle\forall\pi_{1}\mathpunct{.}\exists\pi_{2}\mathpunct{.}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{1}.\exists\beta_{2}\stutter\pi_{2}\mathpunct{.}\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\big(\bigwedge_{a\in\mathit{Obs}}a_{\beta_{1}}=a_{\beta_{2}}\big).

That is, for any execution
\pi_{1}
in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, there exists some execution
\pi_{2}
in
\mathcal{T}_{2}
that,
up to stuttering
, agrees on the values of all observable variables.
We can prove
\varphi_{\mathit{refine}}
by finding a
stutter simulation

(
Namjoshi 1997
;
Namjoshi and Tabajara 2020
)
:
As long as either of the systems makes a “tau-step”, i.e., a step that does not change any variable in
\mathit{Obs}
and thus does not emit a “visible event”, the other system is allowed to stutter.
Our game-based technique can thus be used as a principled method for proving stuttering refinement.
In this light, we can see our game-based verification method as an asynchronous extension of synchronous simulation games; at least in the very special case of

φ
refine

.
Notably, our game-based method – when applied to

φ
refine

– naturally allows both copies to be stuttered independently (as long as the stuttering is progressed
infinitely often
on both traces), i.e., our technique does not require
synchronous progress
where both systems eventually progress
together
(cf.
(
Cho et al. 2023
)
).

Interactive Verification of Infinite-State Systems.

In our implementation, we focused on finite-state systems in order to evaluate our method against existing approaches.
All our results (including soundness and completeness) also apply to infinite-state systems.
While we could attempt to construct and solve the resulting infinite-state game automatically (see, e.g.,
(
Laveaux et al. 2022
;
Beyene et al. 2014
;
Heim and Dimitrova 2024
;
Farzan and Kincaid 2018
;
de Alfaro et al. 2001
;
Samuel et al. 2021
;
Schmuck et al. 2024
)
), the more promising direction is to aim for an interactive proof method.

Correnson and Finkbeiner 2025
recently used the game-based approach for
synchronous
HyperLTL
(
Coenen et al. 2019b
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2022a
)
to develop an
interactive
proof system to verify
\forall\exists
properties in infinite-state software.
Intuitively, their approach uses co-induction to incrementally unfold the underlying game and let the user implicitly define a strategy.
Our game-based approach can handle asynchronous hyperproperties that quantify over stutterings, but still yields a plain two-player game.
This game enables a clear verification objective for the user and provides the much-needed abstraction from infinite traces and stutterings; the user only needs to reason about stuttering in the current state.
Our game then allows us to employ frameworks similar to those used by
Correnson and Finkbeiner 2025
to let the user interactively prove A-HLTL properties by incrementally unfolding the game arena.
Our work thus lays the foundation for developing a unified proof system that can handle many prominent information-flow policies in reactive systems.

9.
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper, we have presented a principled approach for the (automated or interactive) verification of asynchronous hyperproperties expressed in A-HLTL.
Our method approximates quantifier alternation as a game and seamlessly supports quantification over stutterings.
This allows for sound verification of arbitrary
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
A-HLTL properties, well beyond the fragments supported by previous methods.
Moreover, we have shown that our method is complete for many fragments and thus constitutes a finite-state decision procedure.

Our work leaves numerous exciting directions for future extension.
On the theoretical side, one can, e.g., extend our game-based view to support A-HLTL formulas beyond the
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
fragment by incorporating incomplete information
(
Reif 1984
)
; intuitively, the verifier should not base its decision on universal traces and stutterings quantified
after
the (existentially quantified) objects controlled by the verifier
(
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2025b
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner 2025c
)
.
On the practical side, we consider the verification of infinite-state (software) systems to be of particular interest.
As we argued above, our game-based methods (and proofs) also apply to (symbolically represented)
infinite-state
systems, yielding infinite-state games.
Exploring effective techniques to solve the resulting (now infinite-state) game and developing abstraction techniques for a (possibly unbounded) window of states are challenging open problems.
More concretely, it is interesting to apply the interactive proof system of
Correnson and Finkbeiner 2025
to our game, allowing the user to verify asynchronous hyperproperties in infinite-state reactive systems interactively.

Acknowledgements.

This work was supported by the European Research Council (ERC) Grant HYPER (101055412), and by the German Research Foundation (DFG) as part of TRR 248 (389792660).

Appendix A
A-HLTL Syntax from
Baumeister et al. 2021

The original variant of A-HLTL proposed by
Baumeister et al. 2021
, differs from the variant studied in this paper.
In particular, our variant directly quantifies over stutterings of traces and avoids the notation of so-called trajectories.
In this section, we recap the variant of
Baumeister et al. 2021
and show that our variant is equally expressive.

A.1.
A-HLTL by
Baumeister et al. 2021

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a set of trace variables and
\mathcal{W}=\{\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots\}
be a set of trajectory variables.
We define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}}:=\{x_{\pi,\tau}\mid x\in\mathcal{X},\pi\in\mathcal{V},\tau\in\mathcal{W}\}
, i.e., we index the system variables with a trace variable and a trajectory variable.
A-HLTL formulas are defined by the following grammar

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle:=\exists\pi\mathpunct{.}\varphi\mid\forall\pi\mathpunct{.}\varphi\mid\phi

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle:=\text{E}\tau\mathpunct{.}\phi\mid\text{A}\tau\mathpunct{.}\phi\mid\psi

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle:=\theta\mid\neg\psi\mid\psi\land\psi\mid\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\psi\mid\psi\ltlU\psi

where
\pi\in\mathcal{V}
,
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
is a trajectory, and
\theta\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}}}
.
We first quantify over traces (in category
\varphi
), followed by secondary quantification of trajectories (in category
\phi
), followed by an LTL body
\psi
.
Intuitively, each trace-trajectory pair
(\pi,\tau)
describes a (fair) stuttering of trace
\pi
.
The atomic formulas within the LTL body are first-order formulas
\theta\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}}}
, i.e., formulas over indexed system variables.
Different from our definition, the variables are thus not indexed by a stuttering variable, but by a pair of trace and trajectory.
Here, each variable
x_{\pi,\tau}
refers to the value of variable
x
on trace
\pi
under trajectory (stuttering)
\tau
.
The main difference to our variant is thus that by quantifying over a single trajectory
\tau
, we implicitly quantify stutterings for all traces at the same time, i.e.,
(\pi_{1},\tau),\ldots,(\pi_{n},\tau)
all denote (
independent
) stutterings of traces
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
, respectively.
Our variant of A-HLTL makes this explicit by quantifying over a stuttering for each trace individually.

We assume that an A-HLTL formula is closed, i.e., for all atomic formulas
\theta
used in the LTL body
\psi
and any variable
x_{\pi,\tau}
used in
\theta
, the trace variable
\pi
and trajectory variable
\tau
are bound by some outer quantifier.

Semantics.

Baumeister et al. 2021
define the A-HLTL semantics in terms of explicit
trajectories
, guiding stuttering of traces.
We use a simpler (but equivalent) semantics that avoids trajectories and instead directly reasons about stuttering expansions of traces, following
Bozzelli et al. 2022
.

Let
\mathbb{T}\subseteq(\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{V})^{\omega}
be a set of traces.
In the semantics of A-HLTL, we maintain a
trace assignment

\Pi:\mathcal{V}\rightharpoonup(\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{V})^{\omega}
mapping trace variables to traces (used to evaluate trace quantification).
We say a trace assignment
\Pi^{\prime}
is a stuttering of
\Pi
iff
\Pi^{\prime}(\pi)\stutter\Pi(\pi)
for every
\pi\in\mathcal{V}
.
We write
\mathit{FSE}(\Pi)
for the set of all stutterings of
\Pi
.
To handle trajectories, we use a higher-order
trajectory assignment

\Delta:\mathcal{W}\rightharpoonup(\mathcal{V}\rightharpoonup(\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{V})^{\omega})
, mapping trajectory variables to trace assignments.
Intuitively,
\Delta(\tau)
is the stuttered trace assignment under trajectory
\tau
.
Given
\Delta
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\pi\in\mathcal{V}
,
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
, and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the value of variable
x_{\pi,\tau}
in the
i
th step is thus
\Delta(\tau)(\pi)(i)(x)
, i.e., the value of indexed variable
x_{\pi,\tau}
is defined as the value of variable
x
in the
i
th on the trace bound to
\pi
in the trace assignment bound to
\tau
.
Given a trajectory assignment
\Delta
and position
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the extended variable evaluation
\Delta_{(i)}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}}\to\mathbb{V}
by
\Delta_{(i)}(x_{\pi,\tau}):=\Delta(\tau)(\pi)(i)(x)
.
We can now define the semantics as

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\exists\pi\mathpunct{.}\varphi

iff

\displaystyle\exists t\in\mathbb{T}\mathpunct{.}\Pi[\pi\mapsto t],\Delta,i\models_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\forall\pi\mathpunct{.}\varphi

iff

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{T}\mathpunct{.}\Pi[\pi\mapsto t],\Delta,i\models_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\text{E}\tau\mathpunct{.}\phi

iff

\displaystyle\exists\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{FSE}(\Pi)\mathpunct{.}\Pi,\Delta[\tau\mapsto\Pi^{\prime}],i\models_{\mathbb{T}}\phi

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\text{A}\tau\mathpunct{.}\phi

iff

\displaystyle\forall\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathit{FSE}(\Pi)\mathpunct{.}\Pi,\Delta[\tau\mapsto\Pi^{\prime}],i\models_{\mathbb{T}}\phi

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\theta

iff

\displaystyle\Delta_{(i)}\models^{\mathfrak{T}}\theta

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\neg\psi

iff

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i\not\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}

iff

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{1}\text{ and }\Pi,\Delta,i\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{2}

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\psi

iff

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i+1\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi

\displaystyle\Pi,\Delta,i

\displaystyle\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{1}\ltlU\psi_{2}

iff

\displaystyle\exists j\geq i\mathpunct{.}\Pi,\Delta,j\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{2}\text{ and }\forall i\leq k<j\mathpunct{.}\Pi,\Delta,k\models_{\mathbb{T}}\psi_{1}.

We first populate a trace assignment
\Pi
by following the trace-quantifier prefix and adding traces to
\Pi
.
For each trajectory, we then consider FSEs of
\Pi
(quantified universally or existentially) and add them to
\Delta
.
Finally, we can evaluate the LTL body following the usual evaluation of boolean and temporal operators.
Here, an atomic formula
\theta
holds in step
i
if the variable assignment
\Delta_{(i)}
(which assigns values to the indexed variables in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}}
) satisfies
\theta
(modulo theory
\mathfrak{T}
).

A.2.
From Trajectories to Stutterings

It is easy to see that our variant of A-HLTL is equivalent to trajectory-based variant of
(
Baumeister et al. 2021
;
Hsu et al. 2023
)
.
Intuitively, our definition is more verbose by explicitly quantifying over stutterings for each trace, whereas the trajectory-based variant uses a single trajectory to quantify over stutterings for all traces.
We show both directions separately.

Given a trajectory-based formula

\displaystyle\mathds{Q}_{1}\pi_{1}\ldots\mathds{Q}_{n}\pi_{n}.\text{Q}_{1}\tau_{1}\ldots\text{Q}_{k}\tau_{k}\mathpunct{.}\psi

where
\mathds{Q}_{1},\ldots,\mathds{Q}_{n}\in\{\forall,\exists\}
and
\text{Q}_{1},\ldots,\text{Q}_{k}\in\{\text{A},\text{E}\}
we can obtain by replacing each trace trajectory pair
(\pi_{i},\tau_{j})
with a fresh stuttering variable
\beta_{\pi_{i},\tau_{j}}
.
Formally, we define the set of stuttering variables
\mathcal{B}:=\{\beta_{\pi,\tau}\mid\pi\in\mathcal{V},\tau\in\mathcal{W}\}
and consider the A-HLTL formula

\displaystyle\mathds{Q}_{1}\pi_{1}\ldots\mathds{Q}_{n}\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\overline{\text{A}_{1}}\beta_{\pi_{1},\tau_{1}}\stutter\pi_{1}.\cdots\overline{\text{A}_{1}}\beta_{\pi_{n},\tau_{1}}\stutter\pi_{n}.\cdots\overline{\text{A}_{k}}\beta_{\pi_{1},\tau_{k}}\stutter\pi_{1}\cdots\overline{\text{A}_{k}}\beta_{\pi_{n},\tau_{k}}\stutter\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\overline{\psi}

where we define
\overline{\text{A}}:=\forall
and
\overline{\text{E}}:=\exists
to replace trajectory quantification with stuttering quantification.
We define
\overline{\psi}
as the LTL formula obtained form
\psi
by replacing each variable
x_{\pi,\tau}
(indexed by a trace-trajectory pair) with variable
x_{\beta_{\pi,\tau}}
(indexed by a stuttering variable).

Example A.1.

Consider the formula

\displaystyle\forall\pi_{1}.\forall\pi_{2}.\text{E}\tau.\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(o_{\pi_{1},\tau}=o_{\pi_{2},\tau}).

Using our encoding, we translate this to formula

\displaystyle\forall\pi_{1}.\forall\pi_{2}.\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{1}.\exists\beta_{2}\stutter\pi_{2}.\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(o_{\beta_{1}}=o_{\beta_{2}}),

i.e., for each trace-trajectory pair (
(\pi_{1},\tau)
and
(\pi_{2},\tau)
) we simply quantify over a stuttering of that trace.
Note that we write
\beta_{1}
for
\beta_{\pi_{1},\tau}
and
\beta_{2}
for
\beta_{\pi_{2},\tau}
.

\triangle

For each
n\times k
combination of trace-trajectory pair, we simply add an explicit stuttering.
This is more verbose but also highlights much better what is actually happening.
In fact, we believe one of the core reasons why A-HLTL was hard to understand is that a lot of quantification was happening implicitly: Every time we quantify over a trajectory, we quantify over stutterings of
all
traces, even though these stutterings are completely independent.

A.3.
From Stutterings to Trajectories

For the other direction, we simply replace each stuttering quantifier with quantification over a unique trajectory.
Formally, given a stuttering-based A-HLTL formula

\displaystyle\mathds{Q}_{1}\pi_{1}\ldots\mathds{Q}_{n}\pi_{n}.\mathds{Q}^{\prime}_{1}\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{k_{1}}\ldots\mathds{Q}^{\prime}_{m}\beta_{m}\stutter\pi_{k_{m}}\mathpunct{.}\psi

We define the equivalent trajectory-based formula

\displaystyle\mathds{Q}_{1}\pi_{1}\ldots\mathds{Q}_{n}\pi_{n}.\overline{\mathds{Q}^{\prime}_{1}}\tau_{\beta_{1}}\ldots\overline{\mathds{Q}^{\prime}_{m}}\tau_{\beta_{m}}\mathpunct{.}\overline{\psi}

where we define
\overline{\forall}=\text{A}
and
\overline{\exists}=\text{E}
.
We define the new LTL body
\overline{\psi}
as
\psi
, where we replace each variable
x_{\beta}
with
x_{\mathit{base}(\beta),\tau_{\beta}}
.
Each trajectory
\tau_{\beta}
is thus used on a unique trace (namely
\mathit{base}(\beta)
), i.e., instead of quantifying over a stuttering
\beta
of trace
\pi
, we quantify over a trajectory
\tau_{\beta}
(and thus a stuttering of
all
traces) but only use trajectory
\tau_{\beta}
on trace
\pi=\mathit{base}(\beta)
.

Example A.2.

Consider the formula

\displaystyle\forall\pi_{1}.\forall\pi_{2}.\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{1}.\exists\beta_{2}\stutter\pi_{2}.\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(o_{\beta_{1}}=o_{\beta_{2}}),

Using our encoding, we translate this to formula

\displaystyle\forall\pi_{1}.\forall\pi_{2}.\text{E}\tau_{\beta_{1}}.\text{E}\tau_{\beta_{2}}.\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(o_{\pi_{1},\tau_{\beta_{1}}}=o_{\pi_{2},\tau_{\beta_{2}}}).

Note that this encoding is less-optimal (in this example, we could use the
same
trajectory on
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
), but nevertheless yields an equivalent formula.

\triangle

Appendix B
Soundness Proof

In this section, we prove that our game-based method is sound for the verification of Forall-Exist A-HLTL properties by proving
Theorem

5.5
.

See
5.5

To prove
Theorem

5.5
, we distinguish whether
\varphi
contains universally quantified stutterings or not.
We provide a proof for the first part in
Proposition

B.1
and the latter in
Proposition

B.2
.

Proposition B.1.

Assume
\varphi
is a
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
A-HLTL formula without universally quantified stuttering.
If
\mathit{wins}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}}(\mathfrak{V},V_{\mathit{init}})
, then
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
.

Proof.

As
\varphi
contains no universally quantified stuttering, it has the form

\displaystyle\varphi=\forall\pi_{1}\ldots\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\exists\pi_{n+1}\ldots\pi_{n+m}\mathpunct{.}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{l_{1}}\ldots\beta_{k}\stutter\pi_{l_{k}}\mathpunct{.}\psi

where
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n+m\}
.
We assume
\mathit{wins}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}}(\mathfrak{V},V_{\mathit{init}})
, so let
\sigma
be a winning strategy for the verifier in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
.
To show
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
, let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathit{Traces}(\mathcal{T})
be arbitrary traces for the universally quantified trace variables
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
, and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathit{Paths}(\mathcal{T})
be paths that generate
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
(i.e.,
\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{i})=t_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
).
In a first step, we will use
\sigma
to construct traces
t_{n+1},\ldots,t_{n+m}\in\mathit{Traces}(\mathcal{T})
for the existentially quantified
\pi_{n+1},\ldots,\pi_{n+m}
.
For this, consider the initial vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}

\displaystyle v_{0}:=\Big\langle\hbox to9.21pt{\vbox to9.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60332pt\lower-4.60332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.09 0 C 6.09 3.37 3.37 6.09 0 6.09 C -3.37 6.09 -6.09 3.37 -6.09 0 C -6.09 -3.37 -3.37 -6.09 0 -6.09 C 3.37 -6.09 6.09 -3.37 6.09 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40627pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

\displaystyle\big[\pi_{1}\mapsto p_{1}[0,Z-1],\ldots,\pi_{n}\mapsto p_{n}[0,Z-1],\pi_{n+1}\mapsto[s_{0,\mathcal{T}}],\ldots,\pi_{n+m}\mapsto[s_{0,\mathcal{T}}]\big],

\displaystyle\big[\beta_{i}\mapsto 0\big]_{i=0}^{k},\emptyset,q_{0,\psi}\Big\rangle

where each universally quantified trace
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
starts in the initial state window of length
Z
on paths
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, respectively, and
\pi_{n+1},\ldots,\pi_{n+m}
start in the length
1
initial state window.
As
v_{0}\in V_{\mathit{init}}
, strategy
\sigma
wins
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
for
\mathfrak{V}
from
v_{0}
.
Let
V=V_{\mathfrak{V}}\cup V_{\mathfrak{R}}
be the set of vertices in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
.
We now
iteratively
construct a play
\vec{v}\in V^{\omega}
by simulating
\sigma
using the fixed paths
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, i.e., we let the refuter challenge the verifier by always extending the state windows in accordance with
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
.
We will then extract traces
t_{n+1},\ldots,t_{n+m}
from
\sigma
’s response.
To perform this simulation formally, we keep track of indices
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Intuitively, each
c_{i}
tracks the position on
p_{i}
that should be added to
\pi_{i}
’s state window at the next opportunity.

Constructing
\vec{v}

Initially, we set

and

, i.e., the next position that should be added to
\pi_{i}
’s state window is the

.
We then repeat the following (

.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi^{\prime},\mu,\flat,q\rangle
, where

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}:=\big[\pi_{1}\mapsto\Xi(\pi_{1})\cdot p_{1}(c_{1}),\ldots,\pi_{n}\mapsto\Xi(\pi_{n})\cdot p_{n}(c_{n}),\pi_{n+1}\mapsto\Xi(\pi_{n+1}),\ldots,\pi_{n+m}\mapsto\Xi(\pi_{n+m})\big].

That is, for each universally quantified trace
\pi_{i}
, we append state
p_{i}(c_{i})
to
\pi_{i}
’s state window, i.e., we extend the state window in accordance with the fixed path
p_{i}
(using the index
c_{i}
).

Afterward, we append
v^{\prime}
to
\vec{v}
, i.e., define
\vec{v}:=\vec{v}\cdot v^{\prime}
, and repeat.

Constructing
\vec{v}
– END

Let
\vec{v}\in V^{\omega}
be the infinite play computed by the above construction.
It is easy to see that
\vec{v}
is a play in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
that is compatible with
\sigma
.
As
\sigma
is winning for
\mathfrak{V}
, the play
\vec{v}
is won by
\mathfrak{V}
.
By construction of
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
(cf.
Section

5.1
), this implies that the verifier progresses all stutterings (all of which are existentially quantified) infinitely many times.
We will now use
\vec{v}
to
iteratively
construct paths
p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}\in\mathit{Paths}(\mathcal{T})
, defining witness traces for the existentially quantified trace variables
\pi_{n+1},\ldots,\pi_{n+m}
.
Intuitively, we add a state to
p_{i}\in\{p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}\}
, whenever this state was added to the state window of
\pi_{i}
by the verifier.
Formally, For
n<i\leq n+m
, we construct each
p_{i}
as follows:

Constructing
p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}

We initially set
p_{i}:=\epsilon
and
j=0
.
We then repeat the following (
ad infinitum
):

(1)

If
\vec{v}(j)=\langle\hbox to9.21pt{\vbox to9.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60332pt\lower-4.60332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.09 0 C 6.09 3.37 3.37 6.09 0 6.09 C -3.37 6.09 -6.09 3.37 -6.09 0 C -6.09 -3.37 -3.37 -6.09 0 -6.09 C 3.37 -6.09 6.09 -3.37 6.09 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40627pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi,\mu,\flat,q\rangle
and
\mu(\beta)\neq 0
for all
\beta
with
\mathit{base}(\beta)=\pi_{i}
, i.e., all stuttering on
\pi_{i}
have progressed past the first state in
\Xi(\pi_{i})
.
We define
p_{i}:=p_{i}\cdot\Xi(\pi_{i})(0)
, i.e., we extend
p_{i}
with the first position in
\pi_{i}
’s state window.

(2)

Otherwise, we leave
p_{i}
unchanged.

Afterward, we increment
j:=j+1
, and repeat.

Constructing
p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}
– END

As
\sigma
advances all stutterings infinitely often, and we add a state to
p_{i}
iff all stutterings have moved past the first position, we add states to
p_{i}
infinitely many times.
Let
p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}\in\mathit{Paths}(\mathcal{T})
be the resulting
infinite
paths.
Now define trace
t_{i}:=\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{i})
for
n<i\leq n+m
, and let
\Pi:=[\pi_{i}\mapsto t_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}
be the resulting trace assignment.
We claim that
\Pi,\emptyset,0\models_{\mathcal{T}}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{l_{1}}\ldots\beta_{k}\stutter\pi_{l_{k}}\mathpunct{.}\psi
, i.e., traces
t_{n+1},\ldots,t_{n+m}
are valid choices for the existentially quantified trace variables in
\varphi
.
To show this, we need to – for each stuttering
\beta\in\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
– construct a (fair) stuttering of trace
\mathit{base}(\beta)
, that satisfy
\psi
.
To construct these stutterings we, again, use the vertex sequence
\vec{v}
constructed previously.
For each
\beta_{i}\in\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
, we define an infinite state sequence
r_{\beta_{i}}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
, defined, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
as
r_{\beta_{i}}(j):=\Xi(\mathit{base}(\beta_{i}))(\mu(\beta_{i}))
, where
\vec{v}(3\cdot j)=\langle\hbox to9.21pt{\vbox to9.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60332pt\lower-4.60332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.09 0 C 6.09 3.37 3.37 6.09 0 6.09 C -3.37 6.09 -6.09 3.37 -6.09 0 C -6.09 -3.37 -3.37 -6.09 0 -6.09 C 3.37 -6.09 6.09 -3.37 6.09 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40627pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi,\mu,\flat,q\rangle
.
That is, we always look at every third position (i.e., all vertices in the
U
-stage) and use the state that is pointed to by
\mu(\beta_{i})
in state window
\Xi(\mathit{base}(\beta_{i}))
.
First, it is easy to see that for each
\beta_{i}\in\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
,
r_{\beta_{i}}
is a fair stuttering of the path
\Pi(\mathit{base}(\beta_{i}))
(i.e.,
r_{\beta_{i}}\stutter\Pi(\mathit{base}(\beta_{i}))
).
Second, we claim that the stuttered state sequences
\{r_{\beta_{i}}\}
satisfy
\psi
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pi,\big[\beta_{1}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{1}}),\cdots,\beta_{k}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{k}})\big],0\models_{\mathcal{T}}\psi.

This follows from the assumption that
\vec{v}
is won by
\mathfrak{V}
, i.e., the states in each
U
-stage, together, satisfy
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
and, therefore,
\psi
(cf.
Section

5.1
).
Note that in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
, in each
U
-stage, we update
\mathcal{A}_{\psi}
by, for each stuttering
\beta_{i}
, taking the
\mu(\beta_{i})
’s state in
\Xi(\mathit{base}(\beta_{i}))
; just as in the definition of
r_{\beta_{i}}
.
Putting everything together,
\{r_{\beta_{i}}\}
are (fair) stutterings of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n+m}
that satisfy
\psi
.
By the A-HLTL semantics, this already implies that
\Pi,\emptyset,0\models_{\mathcal{T}}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{l_{1}}\ldots\beta_{k}\stutter\pi_{l_{k}}\mathpunct{.}\psi
.
This construction works for arbitrary choices of the universally quantified trace variables
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
in
\varphi
, proving
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
as required.
∎

Proposition B.2.

Assume
\varphi
is a
\forall^{*}\exists^{*}
formula that contains a universally quantified stuttering.
If
\mathit{wins}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}}(\mathfrak{V},V_{\mathit{init}})
, then
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
.

Proof.

We assume that
\mathit{wins}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}}(\mathfrak{V},V_{\mathit{init}})
.
First, we observe that this implies that there exists
at most one
universally quantified stuttering for each trace variable in
\varphi
:
If there are at least two stutterings to the same trace, the verifier can never win, as the refuter can easily enforce a visit to
v_{\mathit{error}}
by letting the two universally quantified stutterings diverge for more than
Z
steps.
So, in the following, we assume that there exists exactly one universally quantified stuttering for each trace variable, i.e.,
\varphi
has the form

\displaystyle\varphi=\forall\pi_{1}\ldots\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\forall\beta^{\prime}_{1}\stutter\pi_{1},\ldots,\beta^{\prime}_{n}\stutter\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{l_{1}}\ldots\beta_{k}\stutter\pi_{l_{k}}\mathpunct{.}\psi.

where
l_{1},\ldots,l_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
That is,
\beta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\beta^{\prime}_{n}
are the fixed universally quantified stutterings of
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
respectively, and
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k}
the existentially quantified stutterings.
Let
\mathcal{T}=(S_{\mathcal{T}},s_{0,\mathcal{T}},\kappa_{\mathcal{T}},\ell_{\mathcal{T}})
and
\mathcal{A}_{\psi}=(Q_{\psi},q_{0,\psi},\delta_{\psi},F_{\psi})
.
We assume that
\sigma
is a winning strategy for the verifier in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
.

To show
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
, let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathit{Traces}(\mathcal{T})
be arbitrary traces for the universally quantified
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
, and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
be paths in
\mathcal{T}
that generates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, i.e.,
\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{i})=t_{i}
.
Let
\Pi:=[\pi_{1}\mapsto t_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}\mapsto t_{n}]
.
Moreover, let
p_{1}^{\prime}\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
be (fair) stutterings of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
(i.e,
p_{i}^{\prime}\stutter p_{i}
), which correspond to the universally quantified stutterings
\beta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
.
We can represent the stutterings as binary sequences
b_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,b_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{\omega}
that dictate when the path is progressed. I.e., for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
b_{i}^{\prime}(j)=\top
iff in the
j
th step
p_{i}^{\prime}
did a non-stuttering step.

To show
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
, we need to find witnesses for the (existentially quantified) stutterings
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}
..
Concretely, for each
\beta_{i}\in\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
, we will construct a sequence
r_{\beta_{i}}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
as stutterings of path
\Pi(\mathit{base}(\beta_{i})
such that

\displaystyle\Pi,\Big[

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{n}^{\prime}),\beta_{1}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{1}}),\ldots,\beta_{k}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{k}})\Big],0\models\psi.

As in the proof of
Proposition

B.1
, we will simulate
\sigma
to construct these stuttered paths.
Consider the initial vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}

\displaystyle v_{0}:=\Big\langle\hbox to9.21pt{\vbox to9.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60332pt\lower-4.60332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.09 0 C 6.09 3.37 3.37 6.09 0 6.09 C -3.37 6.09 -6.09 3.37 -6.09 0 C -6.09 -3.37 -3.37 -6.09 0 -6.09 C 3.37 -6.09 6.09 -3.37 6.09 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40627pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

\displaystyle\big[\pi_{1}\mapsto p_{1}[0,Z-1],\ldots,\pi_{n}\mapsto p_{n}[0,Z-1]\big],\big[\beta\mapsto 0\big]_{\beta\in\{\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}},\emptyset,q_{0,\psi}\Big\rangle

where each universally quantified trace
\pi_{i}
starts in the initial state window of length
Z
on path
p_{i}
.

We now iteratively construct a sequence of vertices
\vec{v}\in V^{\omega}
.
In this sequence we will let the refuter simulate
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and the stutterings
p_{1}^{\prime}\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}
(corresponding to stutterings
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
), and then use
\sigma
’s response to construct witnesses for
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}
.
We keep track of variables
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Again, each
c_{i}
tracks the position on
p_{i}
that currently is in the
last
position of the state window for
\pi_{i}
, which we use to extend the state window in accordance with
p_{i}
.
We give the construction of
\vec{v}
as an algorithm as follows:

Constructing
\vec{v}

Initially, we set
\vec{v}=[v_{0}]
and
c_{1}=\cdots=c_{n}=Z
.
We set
j:=0
.
We then repeat the following (ad infinitum):

Let
v=\vec{v}(j)
be the last vertex in
\vec{v}
.

(1)

If
v=\langle\hbox to9.21pt{\vbox to9.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60332pt\lower-4.60332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.09 0 C 6.09 3.37 3.37 6.09 0 6.09 C -3.37 6.09 -6.09 3.37 -6.09 0 C -6.09 -3.37 -3.37 -6.09 0 -6.09 C 3.37 -6.09 6.09 -3.37 6.09 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40627pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi,\mu,\flat,q\rangle
, we define
v^{\prime}
as the unique successor state of
v
(cf.
Figure

6
).
For all states where the update shifts the window (i.e., the first position is removed, so a position at the back of the window opens), we increment the counters
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
.
Formally, we define
\mathit{mo}=\big\{\pi\mid\forall\beta\mathpunct{.}(\mathit{base}(\beta)=\pi)\Rightarrow\mu(\beta)\neq 0\big\}
as the set of all (universally quantified) trace variables where none of the stutterings in
\varphi
points to the first position (similar to
Figure

6
).
For each
\pi_{i}\in\mathit{mo}
, we define
c_{i}:=c_{i}+1
.

(2)

If
v=\langle\hbox to9.02pt{\vbox to9.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.50859pt\lower-4.50859pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.96 0 C 5.96 3.29 3.29 5.96 0 5.96 C -3.29 5.96 -5.96 3.29 -5.96 0 C -5.96 -3.29 -3.29 -5.96 0 -5.96 C 3.29 -5.96 5.96 -3.29 5.96 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.26389pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi,\mu,\flat,q\rangle
, we define
v^{\prime}:=\sigma(v)
.

(3)

If
v=\langle\hbox to9.02pt{\vbox to9.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.50859pt\lower-4.50859pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.26389pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi,\mu,\flat,q\rangle
, we first define

\displaystyle\Xi^{\prime}:=\big[\pi_{1}\mapsto\Xi(\pi_{1})\cdot p_{1}(c_{1}),\ldots,\pi_{n}\mapsto\Xi(\pi_{n})\cdot p_{n}(c_{n})\big]

i.e., for each universally quantified trace
\pi_{i}
, we append state
p_{i}(c_{i})
, i.e., append the state window in accordance with the fixed path
p_{i}
.
By construction, the counters
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
always point to positions on
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
that are a successor state of the windows
\Xi(\pi_{1}),\ldots,\Xi(\pi_{n})
.
The state we append is thus a valid transition in
\mathcal{T}
.
We define

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}:=\lambda\beta\mathpunct{.}\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} &\mu(\beta)+1\quad&&\text{if }\beta=\beta_{i}^{\prime}\land b_{i}^{\prime}(j)=\top\text{ for some }i\\
&\mu(\beta)\quad&&\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\end{cases}

That is, we progress a universally quantified stuttering
\beta_{i}^{\prime}
(with base path
\pi_{i}
) iff
p_{i}^{\prime}
(the fixed stuttering of
\pi_{i}
) did a non-stuttering step in the
j
th step (as given by
b_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{\omega}
).
This ensures that we update the stuttering pointer
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
such that we reproduce the fixed stuttering
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}
(of paths
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
).
Likewise, we define

\displaystyle\flat^{\prime}:=\flat\cup\{\beta_{i}^{\prime}\mid b_{i}^{\prime}(j)=\top\}

Finally, we define
v^{\prime}:=\langle\hbox to9.02pt{\vbox to9.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.50859pt\lower-4.50859pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.96 0 C 5.96 3.29 3.29 5.96 0 5.96 C -3.29 5.96 -5.96 3.29 -5.96 0 C -5.96 -3.29 -3.29 -5.96 0 -5.96 C 3.29 -5.96 5.96 -3.29 5.96 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.26389pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\flat^{\prime},q\rangle
.
It is easy to see that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
allows a transition from
v
to
v^{\prime}
;
we appended a state to all state windows and (possibly) progressed the universally quantified stuttering
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
. (cf.
Figure

4
).

Afterward, we append
v^{\prime}
to
\vec{v}
, i.e., define
\vec{v}:=\vec{v}\cdot v^{\prime}
.
We then increment
j:=j+1
, and repeat.

Constructing
\vec{v}
– END

Let
\vec{v}\in V^{\omega}
be the infinite play computed by the above construction.
It is easy to see that
\vec{v}
is a play in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
that is compatible with
\sigma
.
As, by assumption,
\sigma
is winning, the play
\vec{v}
is won by
\mathfrak{V}
.
Now by assumption
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}
are
fair
stutterings of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, so the boolean sequences
b_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,b_{n}^{\prime}
contain
\top
infinitely many times.
By construction of
\vec{v}
, the stuttering pointers for
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
are thus also progressed infinitely many times.
This implies that the premise
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mathit{moved}_{\beta_{n}^{\prime}}
of
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
(cf.
Section

5.1
) holds.
As
\vec{v}
is won by
\sigma
, this implies that conclusion of
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
also holds.
In particular,
\sigma
progresses all stutterings
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}
infinitely many times.

We will now use
\vec{v}
to construct stutterings for
\beta\in\{\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
(similar to the proof of
Proposition

B.1
).
For each path
\beta\in\{\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
we define a sequence
r_{\beta}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
as follows:
For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\vec{v}(3\cdot j)=\langle\hbox to9.21pt{\vbox to9.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60332pt\lower-4.60332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.09 0 C 6.09 3.37 3.37 6.09 0 6.09 C -3.37 6.09 -6.09 3.37 -6.09 0 C -6.09 -3.37 -3.37 -6.09 0 -6.09 C 3.37 -6.09 6.09 -3.37 6.09 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40627pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\Xi,\mu,\flat,q\rangle
and define
r_{\beta}(j):=\Xi(\mathit{base}(\beta))(\mu(\beta))
.
That is, we always look at every third position (i.e., all vertices in the
U
-stage) and use the state that is pointed to by
\mu(\beta)
on state window
\Xi(\mathit{base}(\beta))
.
Note that for each
1\leq i\leq n
,
r_{\beta_{i}^{\prime}}=p_{i}^{\prime}
, i.e., the state sequence for the universally quantified stutterings
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
are exactly the fixed stuttering of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
we used in the construction of
\vec{v}
(as we progressed the
\beta_{i}^{\prime}
stutterings in exactly those steps where
p_{i}^{\prime}
did a non-stuttering step on
p_{i}
, i.e., those steps where
b_{i}^{\prime}
was set to true).

It is easy to see that for each
\beta\in\{\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
,
r_{\beta}
is a fair stuttering of the path
\Pi(\mathit{base})(\beta)
(similar to the proof of
Proposition

B.1
).
Moreover, we claim that the paths
\{r_{\beta}\}
satisfy
\psi
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Pi,\Big[

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}),\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{n}^{\prime}}),\beta_{1}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{1}}),\ldots,\beta_{k}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{k}})\Big],0\models\psi.

This directly follows from the assumption that
\vec{v}
is won by
\mathfrak{V}
:
As
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}
are
fair
stutterings, the premise of
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
holds, which – as
\mathcal{A}_{\psi}
tracks
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
– implies that the conclusion of
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
(and in particular
\psi
) also holds (cf.
Section

5.1
).

So
\{r_{\beta}\}
are (fair) stutterings of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
that satisfy
\psi
, and for each
1\leq i\leq n
,
r_{\beta_{i}^{\prime}}
equals the (universally quantified)
p_{i}^{\prime}
.
By the A-HLTL semantics, this implies that

\Pi,\emptyset,0\models\forall\beta^{\prime}_{1}\stutter\pi_{1},\ldots\beta_{n}^{\prime}\stutter\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{l_{1}}\ldots\beta_{k}\stutter\pi_{l_{k}}\mathpunct{.}\psi

as desired.
This construction, works for arbitrary choices of the universally quantified traces
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
and all choices of stutterings
\beta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\beta_{n}^{\prime}
, i.e., for all such choices, we can use
\sigma
to construct stuttering
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}
that satisfy
\psi
.
This proves
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
as required.
∎

See
5.6

Proof.

We show both part separately:

•

Assume
\sigma
is a winning strategy for the verifier in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z}
.
We can easily extend
\sigma
to a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,Z^{\prime}}
: We simply ignore the larger window size, i.e., the strategy only considers the smaller window of size
Z
and ignores all states beyond
Z
for it’s decision.

•

We can extend the ideas from
Example

C.1
.
For each
Z\geq 1
, define
\mathcal{T}_{Z}:=(S,s_{0},\kappa,\ell)
, where
S=\{s_{0},\ldots,s_{Z}\}
,
\kappa(s_{i}):=\{s_{(i+1)\%(Z+1)}\}
, and
\ell(s_{1})=\cdots=\ell(s_{Z-1}):=[a\mapsto 1],\ell(s_{0}):=[a\mapsto 0]
.
Note that
\mathcal{T}_{Z}
generates the unique trace
(\{a\mapsto 0\}\{a\mapsto 1\}^{Z})^{\omega}
.

\mathcal{T}_{2}

Appendix D
Completeness For Alternation-Free Formulas

See
6.1

Proof.

The first direction follows directly from
Theorem

5.5
.
For the other direction, assume that
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
.
We first consider the case where
\varphi
is a
\exists^{*}
formula.
As
\varphi
contains at most one stuttering per trace variable, we can assume, w.l.o.g., that is has the form

\displaystyle\exists\pi_{1}\ldots\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\stutter\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\psi

i.e.,
\beta_{i}
is the unique stuttering on trace
\pi_{i}
.
As
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
, we get witness traces
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathit{Traces}(\mathcal{T})
for
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
generated by paths
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathit{Paths}(\mathcal{T})
(i.e.,
t_{i}=\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{i})
).
Moreover, by the semantics, there exists stuttered state sequences
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
such that
p_{i}^{\prime}\stutter p_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and

\displaystyle[\pi_{1}\mapsto t_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}\mapsto t_{n}],\big[\beta_{1}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,\beta_{n}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{n}^{\prime})\big],0\models_{\mathcal{T}}\psi

The verifier can now easily win
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,1}
by simply appending states according to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, and stuttering such that the current state at the
i
th visit to the
U
-stage is
p_{1}^{\prime}(i),\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}(i)
.
This is similar to the strategy used in the proof of
Theorem

5.5
.
Note that the refuter has no influence in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,1}
, i.e., each vertex controlled by
\mathfrak{R}
has a unique successor vertex.
As
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}
are fair stutterings of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
the verifier progresses
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
infinity often.
As
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{n}^{\prime}
satisfy
\psi
, the resulting play is won by the verifier, so
\mathit{wins}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,1}}(\mathfrak{V},V_{\mathit{init}})
as required.

The case where
\varphi
is a
\forall^{*}
formula is similar.
For this,

\displaystyle\varphi=\forall\pi_{1}\ldots\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\forall\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}\stutter\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\psi

Here, the verifier has no choice in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,1}
, i.e., each vertex controlled by
\mathfrak{V}
has a unique successor vertex.
However, the transitions rules of the refuter (cf.
Figure

4
) only allow him to use some path combination
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}
and stutter those paths.
The construction of
\psi_{\mathit{mod}}
(cf.
Section

5.1
), ensures that the verifier wins if the stuttering in unfair (i.e., some path is stuttered forever).
If the stuttering is fair, all path combinations satisfy
\psi
as we assumed
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
.
∎

Appendix E
Completeness For Terminating System

E.1.
Proof of
Theorem

6.2

See
6.2

Proof.

The “only if” direction follows directly from
Theorem

5.5
.
For the other direction, assume that
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
and let
\varphi=\forall\pi_{1}\ldots\pi_{n}\mathpunct{.}\exists\pi_{n+1}\ldots\pi_{n+m}\mathpunct{.}\exists\beta_{1}\stutter\pi_{l_{1}}\ldots\beta_{k}\stutter\pi_{l_{k}}\mathpunct{.}\psi
.
We sketch a strategy for the verifier in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,D}
, showing that
\mathit{wins}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,D}}(\mathfrak{V},V_{\mathit{init}})
.
Each initial vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,D}
assigns the (universally quantified) traces
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
to a state window of length
D
, i.e., a sequence of
D
consecutive states in
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{D}
be those initial path fragments.
By the assumption that
\mathcal{T}
is terminating with depth
D
, each prefix
y_{i}
already defines a unique path (i.e., ends in a sink state).
Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathit{Paths}(\mathcal{T})
be the unique paths in
\mathcal{T}
obtained by extending
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
.
Now instate the universally quantified trace variables
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
in
\varphi
with traces
\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{1}),\ldots,\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{n})
.
As
\mathcal{T}\models\varphi
, we thus get witness traces
t_{n+1},\ldots,t_{n+m}\in\mathit{Traces}(\mathcal{T})
for the existentially quantified trace variables
\pi_{n+1},\ldots,\pi_{n+m}
, and corresponding paths
p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}\in\mathit{Paths}(\mathcal{T})
(i.e.,
\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(p_{i})=t_{i}
for
n<i\leq n+m
).
Moreover, for each
\beta_{i}\in\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}
, there exists a stuttering
r_{\beta_{i}}\in S_{\mathcal{T}}^{\omega}
of path
p_{l_{i}}
(i.e.,
r_{\beta_{i}}\stutter p_{l_{i}}
), such that

\displaystyle\big[\pi_{i}\mapsto t_{i}\big]_{\pi_{i}\in\{\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n+m}\}},\big[\beta_{i}\mapsto\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(r_{\beta_{i}})\big]_{\beta_{i}\in\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}},0\models_{\mathcal{T}}\psi.

The strategy for the verifier in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{T},\varphi,D}
now simply extends the state windows of
\pi_{n+1},\ldots,\pi_{n+m}
according to
p_{n+1},\ldots,p_{n+m}
, and stutters
\beta_{i}
according to
r_{\beta_{i}}
(similar to the refuter in the proof of
Theorem

5.5
).
Note that a window of size
D
suffices, even though we deal with multiple stutterings on the same trace: After progressing a stuttering pair
D
times, the state never changes (as we reach a sink state after at most
D
steps).
As long as some stuttering pointer has not reached a sink state, the verifier can thus stutter according to
\{r_{\beta_{i}}\}
without letting any stuttering pointer grow strictly larger than
D
.
∎
